3. 3 Sustainable development potential :
19.
This project contributes to the Sustainable development goal (SDG) target 7.1, by 2030
ensure universal access to affordable, reliable, and modern energy service, through directly
addressing one of the indicators, share of the population using modern cooking solutions (by
4
urban/rural). According to IEA (2019) , data shows that as of 2018, the proportion of the world
population with access to clean cooking is 65 per cent. As of the same year, the proportion of the
population in developing countries with access to clean cooking was 56 per cent. Nepal remains
at 30 per cent despite its continuous increase since the baseline year of 2000. This project not
only targets to increase access to clean cooking solutions, but also aims to ensure continued use
of these solutions at least 90 percent of targeted households.
20.
The project has a high potential to generate health benefits by reducing indoor air
pollution (IAP) while reducing fuelwood consumption. As of 2019, according to the World
5
Health Organization , 69 per cent of population in Nepal rely primarily on polluting fuels and
technologies for cooking. The use of solid fuels and kerosene in households is associated with
increased mortality from acute lower respiratory, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease,
stroke, ischaemic heart disease, and lung cancer. IAP has been identified as the top-fourth risk
6
factor driving overall morbidity and mortality in developing countries . In Nepal, approximately
7
8,700 people die each year from exposure to IAP . Studies have shown that the adoption of
cleaner fuels and levels of IAP are dependent on household’s socioeconomic status and
geographical regions. A study shows burning biomass fuel for daily activities such as cooking
and heating is common in Nepal, especially among rural households, and that the majority
Nepalese households use cheaper, solid (unclean) fuels (such as wood, dung and crop residues)
which are cheaper and more widely available. The study also shows that the provinces, where
poverty intensity and the percentage of the population in severe poverty are high, also showed
8
hig h level of exposure to IAP .
21.
Women and children will be primarily benefitting from the reduction in IAP that this
project will achieve. It is estimated that Tier 3+ improved cookstoves can reduce fuel wood
consumption by 30 per cent on average and will reduce more than 30 per cent of IAP. The
project will monitor and report on the improvement in health and safety as a result of reduced
ind oor smoke.
22.
The project has a high potential to generate socio-economic benefits also. Reducing
firewood consumption for traditional cookstoves means that women will spend less time for
collecting firewood and cooking, which in turn increase their ability to participate in income-
generating activities. The project aims to reduce 39.41 per cent of expenses spent for fuel woods
by the end of the project, in comparison to the baseline identified during project year 1. Increase
in local employment in CCS sector is expected, especially in rural areas. One of the project
activities is to conduct skills development training to CCS beneficiaries and vulnerable and
disadvantaged groups. In total of 1,200 persons will receiving the skill training on making fuel
for Tier 3+ ICS, repair and main tenance, biomass fuel processing, biogas installation, electrical

23.
Another economic benefit at a national level is in the reduction of trade deficit by
reducing LPG imports. The project is expected to reduce LPG imports by about 30,000 tonnes,
and saving foreign currency amounting to USD 17.5 million each year.

3. 4 Needs of the recipient
24.
Nepal is a least developed country. Terai, the project location, has a very high population
density, accounting for 50 per cent of the total population, with extreme pressure on forests.
Previous to this project, the AE distributed mud ICS which had immediate positive impacts, but
the durability of mud ICS did not provide a long-lasting impact. Promotion of clean cooking
solutions with increased reliability and durability is essential. Training opportunities on CCS
technologies would be extended to primarily women and vulnerable and disadvantage groups,
including widows, single women, women headed households, minor ethnic groups, low caste
pe ople (Dalit) and indigenous group (Janiati).
25.
The project has defined beneficiary selection criteria at a high level. The criteria include
consideration of income category, affordability factor, access to electricity, demands from the
local governments, and quality assurance of technologies. The selection criteria will be defined
in detail during the project implementation stage while preparing the standard operating
pr ocedure/operational manual for the project.
26.
Under the new Constitution of 2015, many government functions were devolved to the
(newly established) provinces and local government bodies. These lower levels of government
are generally lacking in capacity to apply integrated approaches into regional planning,
including climate change forecasts. This project will target 150 local governments, out of the
total of 287 local governments in the Terai region. The AE will sign Memorandum of
Understanding (MOU) with the provincial governments and the local governments to facilitate
development and reinforcement of CCS policies and strategies. The participating local
governments will be selected based on demands received during the project implementation
stage.
Scale: N/A
3. 5 Country ownership
9
27.
In its second Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) submitted in December 2020 ,
Ne pal had indicated its priority in residential cooking and biogas as the following:
(a)
By 2030, ensure 25% of households use electric stoves as their primary mode of
cooking;
(b)
By 2025, install 500,000 improved cookstoves, specifically in rural areas; and
(c)
By 2025, install an additional 200,000 household biogas plants and 500 large scale
biogas plants (institutional/industrial/municipal/community).
28.
These three targets will contribute to emissions reduction of 11% from cooking sector
by 2025, from 1,999 GgCO2eq in BAU (baseline set in 2011) to approx., 1,774 GgCO2eq.
29.
The project is in strategic alignment with the NDC. It meets 90 per cent of the target for
improved cookstoves and 36 per cent of electricity goals. With increased generation of
electricity, having electricity as a main avenue of cooking is in alignment with the Government’s

energy security strategy. Whilst GCF Country Programme for Nepal has not been yet approved,
the project has been identified as a priority of the Government given its strong linkages with the
Government led larger Terai Clean Cooking Programme, which was announced in the first year
(2019/20) of the Government’s 15th five-year plan, with an aim to replace traditional fuels with
tie r 3 or better cook stoves, modern biogas cookstoves in 22 districts of the Terai region.
30.
The funding proposal was developed with full consultation among all relevant

3. 6 Efficiency and effectiveness
31.
The project exhibits high efficiency per USD of GCF funding in comparison with the
2
relevant benchmarks. The project’s mitigation cost-effectiveness is at USD 3.24/Mt CO which is
well below comparable GCF portfolio projects, well below DAEs’ average, and well below
2
international benchmarks for clean cooking projects (USD 40.92/t CO according to the IEA
2020). The funding amount requested by the AE is thoroughly justified. One of the main
strengths of the proposal is a granular analysis of both financing gaps and economic and
financial considerations for the interventions under consideration, where the AE provided a
detailed description of (1) the allocation of each funding component to each technology, and (2)
the unit cost of each technology in the Economic and Financial Analysis.
32.
The economic analysis shows that the project will deliver high returns at society level
with an estimated 31 per cent Economic Internal Rate of Return (IRR) well above the 9 per cent
social discount rate used in the analysis. The economic benefits are balanced between emissions
reductions, health benefits and cost savings. These results are robust to the sensitivity analysis
de veloped by the AE.
33.
The profitability analysis shows that investment in the clean cooking solutions under
consideration would not happen without the support of GCF. The AE developed three scenarios:
(1) a scenario where investment takes place without the reverse auction mechanism and
without GCF; (2) a scenario where only the reverse auction scenario is considered and (3) a
thi rd scenario including GCF support.
34.
First, improved cookstove technology shows a negative net present value in the absence
of GCF support and in the reverse auction case; the technology becomes profitable with GCF
support with not only a good IRR of 115 per cent, but also a reasonable one year payback period
suitable for a household investment time horizon. For the electric cookstove technology, the
picture is more nuanced. The IRR of the technology is high enough without the GCF’s
intervention in most of the cases considered by the AE (combinations of assumptions);
however, the payback period is too high for a household to invest in the technology. With GCF’s
support, the technology’s payback period drops from an initial 2.4 years to a more suitable 1.3
ye ars.
35.
In addition to a thorough profitability analysis, the AE also performed a complementary
analysis exploring the impact of the investments on household income. Without GCF support,
investing in improved cookstoves or electric cookstoves would account for between 7 and 120
pe r cent of household income.
36.
The AE will deliver the support through local governments and stakeholders. Such
delivery model will contribute to the development of local value chains for clean cooking.
Combining the GCF grant with this choice of delivery model, the project seems well positioned
not only to deliver the intended impact, but also induce a real paradigm shift that will unlock
pr ivate sector financing.
37.
Based on the financial analysis, the GCF intervention is not crowding out the private
sector. It will ultimately lead to a reduction of costs of technologies as well as an increased

=== PAGE 124 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 04
Page 124
awareness on the benefits and technology service of clean cooking solutions. In addition,
increasing electric cookstove reliability and developing local value chains will further increase
the adoption of the technology with opportunities for private sector financing.
38.
The capacity-building components will cement the impact of the project and increase
the likelihood of improved access to finance for clean cooking solutions in Nepal.
IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies
4. 1 Environmental and social safeguards
39.
The project is classified as Category C by the AE which the Secretariat confirms and that
it is within the AE’s accreditation level. While the project is geared towards having positive
outcomes, potential minor site-specific negative environmental impacts may arise from
improper disposal of used cookstove components as well as that of the bioslurry from the
operation of the domestic biogas plants. A management plan is put in place to use the bioslurry
as organic fertilizer and is codified in an Environmental and Social Action Plan. The project will
likewise promote the reuse and recycling of used/damaged cookstove components while non-
recyclables will be disposed of in accordance with existing municipal waste management
requirements. Labour issues concerning the vendors/suppliers of the improved cookstoves in
relation to avoiding the potential for child and forced labour and occupational health and safety
risks to workers will be indicated in contractual agreements. The AE will ensure that these risks
are avoided and appropriately mitigated.
40.
The environmental co-benefits of the project include reduced indoor air pollution,
improved air quality and reduced forest degradation as the improved cookstoves/electric
cookstoves and the biogas systems are expected to replace the use of fuelwood and cow dung.
The use of cow dung as organic manure is also expected to improve soil quality. The social co-
benefits include increased resilience through improved health as a result of decreased indoor
air pollution and improved household air quality.
41.
The project documents state that the major beneficiaries include the Tharu people of
Terai and indicate that there will be no adverse impacts to Janajati or indigenous peoples
communities. The gender assessment and action plan states that Janajati will be prioritized in
the selection of beneficiary households, among economically impoverished and socially
ex cluded families.
42.
The project has documented and provided a summary of the consultations conducted.
The AE notes limited initial outreach due to the COVID-19 pandemic and consultations with
indigenous and Dalit organizations were conducted via email and telephone. A Stakeholder
Engagement Plan (SEP) is also developed which lays down the approach for stakeholder
engagement during implementation. It identifies the resources required and the corresponding
timing and responsibilities. The SEP provides for the engagement principles, requirements and
regulations pertaining to stakeholder consultations and includes establishment of a grievance
redress mechanism that allows for complaints to be raised both at the entity and activity levels
in addition to having access to the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism. In line with the GCF
Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF indigenous peoples focal point will be available for
assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.
4. 2 Gender policy
43.
The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore
complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.

=== PAGE 125 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 04
Page 125
44.
The gender assessment describes the enabling environment for the pursuit of gender
equity and gender mainstreaming in Nepal. The Constitution of Nepal commits to ending gender
discrimination and supports equal rights to property, reproductive health rights, and
proportional representation at all levels of governance as well as the right to enjoy fundamental
human rights for all women. The country has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All
forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Optional Protocol to CEDAW. In
2015, Nepal introduced the Sexual Harassment at Workplace Prevention Act and in 2017 the
Criminal Code introduced measures to protect women, children and minorities. In 1980 Nepal
created a Women in Development office to support an equitable and prosperous society with
strategies to mainstream gender, empower women and facilitate inclusion in governance and
development. Over the years this has been supported through the creation of the Ministry of
Women, Children and Senior Citizens and a National Commission for Women, and a number of
national programmes and development strategies. Gender mainstreaming policies and support
in the energy sector has lagged behind until more recently. In 2013, the Renewable Energy
Subsidy Policy and Rural Energy Subsidy Delivery Mechanism were developed and have specific
subsidies targeted to women and the socially excluded groups of households with single
women, disaster victims, people affected by conflict and endangered groups. The National Rural
Renewable Energy Program (2012–2017) mandated affirmative action aimed at building
capacity and empowering women and marginalized groups in the energy sector. There are also
community-level groups and networks that are supporting gender equity, such as FECOFUN a
network of Community Forest User Groups, and the Women’s Network of Energy and
En vironment.
45.
Despite the existence of the enabling environment women still have less access to
information, training and capacity-building than men. The implementation of policy
commitments is lagging, and the leadership role of women is undermined in the energy sector
as well as across other sectors by prevalent gender norms and discriminatory practices.
Women’s access to finance is also inhibited by cultural norms, limited access to land and the
inability of women to present assets for collateral purposes. The barriers exist not only in the
policy and legal framework but also in the institutions, programmes and implementation of
int erventions in the energy and water sectors.
46.
The gender assessment was undertaken on the basis of a desk review while also
engaging stakeholders such as government agencies, civil society representatives, members of
networks of women rights groups and gender experts. It also has consulted with the private
sec tor in both the supply and demand side, including those led by women.
47.
The gender assessment identifies that the engagement and involvement of women in
technical and expertise levels in the energy sector is very limited. Access to information and
training opportunities, in addition to lower educational attainment and prevalent
discriminatory gender roles, contribute towards the limited number of women experts in the
sector as well as their inability to be in decision-making positions. On the other hand, the
assessment also identifies that women are one of the most vulnerable groups, socially expected
to be involved more around household activities, spending 25–50 per cent more time on
household tasks, economic and agricultural activities than men. Land and house ownership by
fem ales is very low compared with men despite the existence of registration fee waivers.
48.
There is a relatively high rate of gender-based violence in Nepal and this is particularly
high in Province 2, which is a focal area of the project, although the project does not anticipate
any escalation as a result of project intervention. Barriers to equal representation up and down
the value chains in the energy sector include traditional social norms biased against women,
poverty as a result of limited access to education, early marriage, and limited asset ownership.
Furthermore, women’s participation is limited in decision-making and income generating
activities, and the technical workforce.

=== PAGE 126 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 04
Page 126
49.
It is noted that women play a significant role in cooking, and bear the negative effects of
fuel collection (142 minutes per day spent on collecting firewood compared with 52 minutes
per day by men), transportation, air pollution in the home, time consumption and unsafe
cooking technologies. Despite the central role played by women, they are not generally involved
in selecting cookstoves. Often, the poorest and most vulnerable communities cannot purchase
the tier 3+ improved cookstoves. Even if the stoves are distributed free of cost, fuelwood is
another major challenge. Most of the communities use cow dung as fuel, which is not compatible
with the available clean stove technologies. Clean technologies compatible with available local
fuel sources are essential so that the most vulnerable people can assess the technologies,
reducing their exposure to indoor air pollution, improving health and reducing the workload of
wo men.
50.
The AE has provided a gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements
of the Gender Policy of the GCF. The gender action plan includes activities that address
challenges faced by women and includes a baseline, indicators, targets, timelines and budgets.
The project will hire a gender expert to ensure gender issues are integrated and addressed
throughout the project and implementation of the gender action plan. The AE will also be
conducting further consultations and collecting additional baseline information in the first
phase of the project. The activities included in the gender action plan address the supply and
demand side of the gender issues discussed. It includes activities to raise awareness on gender
issues in the sector as well as to create an inclusive enabling environment. The project is also
expected to create employment and volunteering opportunities for women. Skills training and
opportunities to participate in technical training will be made available to women and women
owned and managed enterprises, and at the same time the AE will be taking measures to ensure
that the barriers to women’s participation will be minimized. Gender mainstreaming expertise
will be made available and integrated into the project’s decision-making bodies, training
materials, publications, communication tools, policies, management and monitoring and
evaluation. The AE is also taking specific measures to ensure gender inclusiveness in the design
of normative policies and plans, with strengthened representation from women. Gender
mainstreaming training will be provided to local and provincial institutions to change policies
an d practices to provide more equity in employment opportunities and benefit sharing.
51.
While the AE believes there is a low risk of increased gender-based violence, it is
strongly encouraged to develop and articulate the specific mechanism(s) that the project will
put in place to provide women with easy and safe access to grievance and redress mechanisms
in case instances of gender-based violence do occur.
44.3.3.1. ROvisekrasl l project assessment (medium risk)
52.
The main objective of the project is to increase the use of clean cooking solutions by: (i)
bulk tendering via reverse auctioning for cost effectiveness; (ii) output-based financing for
cookstoves; and (iii) mainstreaming and capacitating local governments in the renewable
energy (RE) sector. The total project cost is USD 49.1 million, of which GCF will contribute USD
21 .1 million, AEPC USD 20.9 million and local governments USD 7 million.
53.
The majority of the project costs (USD 39.8 million) will be for the procurement and
installation of 500,000 electric cookstoves, 490,000 Tier 3+ improved cookstoves and 10,000
biogas plants. For strengthening the enabling environment USD 0.5 million is allocated, about
US 6 million for institution and supply chain capacity development and USD 2.4 million for
project management costs. Though Nepal is an LDC, the relatively high IRR for electric
cookstoves can support the partial use of a reimbursable grant. However, the accreditation
scope of the AE constrains the use of a non-reimbursable grant in the funding proposal.

=== PAGE 127 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 04
Page 127
4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project
(medium to high risk)
54.
AEPC is the AE and the EE for the project. AEPC, under the Ministry of Energy, Water
Resources and Irrigation, is the national focal agency for the promotion of renewable energy
technologies in Nepal. AEPC has implemented projects and programmes in the area of climate
change mitigation and adaptation, RE promotion and low-carbon economic development worth
more than USD 328 million up until 2017. This project is AEPC’s first project with GCF. AEPC has
pr ovided no information on projects implemented with donor funds since 2017.
55.
AEPC will engage with commercial parties (vendors) through bulk tendering and
reverse auctioning for services for the clean cookstoves. AEPC has created a project governance
with regional project management units and a project implementation unit within AEPC. The
implementation unit is guided by the project management committee chaired by the Executive
Director of AEPC. To achieve stable adoption of the cookstoves by the end users, it is advised
t 4 h .3 a .3 t . theP ArEo jdeocets-s ap qeucaifliicty e cxheeccukt ioonn t rhies kves n(dmoerds’i suump prliiseks) a long with focusing on price reduction.
56.
Demand risk: below-expected offtake of cookstoves would proportionally reduce the
impact of the project. For the electric cookstoves and Tier 3 improved cookstoves, significant
cost savings combined with reduced costs as the result of subsidies are expected to support
demand. For biogas plants, cost savings for the target beneficiaries are low, which could limit
offtake. Also, non-financial behavioural aspects could reduce demand as well as logistical
challenges to supply remote communities. Components 2 and 3 are expected to support demand
via awareness-raising and supply chain support.
57.
Use risk: the impact of the project depends on the stoves being fully utilized during their
life. The project does not require households to discard old stoves and therefore households will
potentially cook on both systems. The need for special fuel or requirements to process fuel for
Tier 3+ improved cookstoves could limit day-to-day adoption. Furthermore, insufficient grid
capacity could hamper continued use of electric cookstoves. It is advised to consider requiring
tha t the old stoves be retired to support continued use of the appliances.
58.
Sustainability risk: the attractiveness of the cooking solutions is supported by a high
level of subsidy of 96 per cent, 89 per cent and 82 per cent of the costs of the Tier 3+ improved
cookstoves, biogas and electric cookstoves, respectively. This subsidy will halt at the end of the
implementation period leading to an approximately fivefold increase in cookstove prices. This
could have demand implications. It is advised to consider a gradual phaseout of the level of
su bsidy to allow for the gradual transition to a market with lower levels of subsidy.
59.
Vendor/supply chain risk: the project presumes significant cost savings as a result of
reverse auctioning. Failed auctions, for example due to high efficiency requirements, could
increase the price of the units and reduce the number of beneficiaries. Underdeveloped supply
chains form a risk for the sustainability of the intervention and successful execution of
co mponents 2 and 3 and is therefore an important driver of success of the project.
60.
Co-financing risk: the co-financing commitment of local governments (USD 7 million) is
pending due to budgetary reasons. Over 150 local governments will provide funds. Co-financing
commitment letters will be a condition for disbursement but a delay in providing such letters

64.
The beneficiary country, Nepal, is not subject to United Nations Security Council
res trictive measures.
65.
The AE will act as the executing entity (EE) for this project, through a dedicated PIU.
Fo ur additional PIUs will be established at the provincial level.
66.
The AE has assessed the money laundering, terrorist financing and prohibited practices
risks and deemed them to be of low probability and high impact. To mitigate the risks, the AE
intends to implement measures required by the national law, including those that pertain to
“know-your-customer” procedures. Additionally, the AE places great emphasis on its
Compliance and Ethics Subcommittee, which is responsible for overseeing the implementation
act ivities during the project’s lifecycle.
67.
The Office of Risk Management and Compliance (ORMC)/Compliance team notes that
the AE as well as one of its implementing partners, Nepal Electricity Authority (NEA), have
encountered difficulties with similar initiatives in the past. These predominantly concerned
project oversight and integrity matters, and on some occasions, allegations of misconduct on the
part of the NEA’s employees. These allegations were investigated by the Commission for
Investigation of Abuse of Authority (CIAA). In particular, ORMC would like to highlight one
instance where the former managing director of NEA was sentenced in 2017 for irregularities
regarding procurement procedures; therefore ORMC/Compliance team recommends
performing enhanced due diligence for all aspects of the project that may require coordination
wi th the said entity.
68.
ORMC/Compliance has conducted a review of the project in accordance with relevant
GCF Board approved policies and, based on available information for this funding proposal, has
determined a risk rating of “High” and has no objection to this request proceeding to the next

4. 4 Fiduciary
71.
AEPC will act as both the AE and the EE and will have a separate PIU to execute the
project. As an AE, AEPC will receive the funds from GCF. A separate bank account will be opened
for the project, and separate financial management guidelines will be developed for the
tra nsparent and effective management of the financial resources.
72.
In its capacity as EE, AEPC will set up a dedicated PIU to oversee and manage the project
implementation under the strategic guidance of the project management committee. The PIU
will prepare an annual work plan and budget, including a procurement plan, which will be
approved by the project management committee. Based on the approval of this plan, PIU will
mobilize and release the funds according to the agreements/contracts to the service providers.
Additionally, the PIU will be responsible for the day-to-day management and monitoring of the
project activities according to approved annual work plans and budgets. Finally, the PIU will
execute the project following the Government of Nepal’s rules and procedures regarding
acq uiring project services, supplies and works.
73.
AEPC will conduct quarterly internal audits in accordance with the Office of the
Financial General Comptroller. Because AEPC is a government entity, the annual audit of AEPC is
under the jurisdiction of the Office of the Auditor General; as such, the Office of the Auditor
General shall conduct the final audit of the project. The Compliance Unit of AEPC will serve the
oversight function to ensure internal control at the institutional level, and the Compliance and
Eth ics Subcommittee will provide the oversight of the unit.
74.
APEC will follow the procurement system and procedures of the Government of Nepal.
Application of Public Procurement Act (PPA) 2063 and Public Procurement Regulation (PPR)
2064 shall be enforced in all aspects of the procurement processes regarding securing products
and services from any third parties by the EE for funds received from GCF and other sources.
4. 5 Results monitoring and reporting
75.
This project is a mitigation activity aimed at the dissemination of CCS in Nepal. Through
the establishment of various facilities and incentive mechanisms, the project aims at inducing
behavioural change and supporting household and other users in Nepal in switching from non-
ren ewable biomass and fossil fuel-based cooking solutions to clean cooking options.
76. 2
The project is expected to prevent GHG emissions amounting to 6.5 MtCO eq over the
2
project lifetime of 24 years, or an average of 271,000 tCO eq per year. The emission reductions
have been estimated by closely following approved methodologies under the clean development
mechanism (CDM), as well as the information on the share of non-renewable biomass in Nepal
reported by the country’s designated national authority to the CDM secretariat. The
additionality analysis has also been conducted, demonstrating the existence of insurmountable
common practice and financial barriers to the dissemination of CCS in Nepal.

4. 6 Legal assessment
81.
The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the AE on 7 January 2020 (the
“A MA”), and it became effective on 19 June 2020.
82.
The AE has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all
internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project. It is
recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board (a) the AE has
obtained all its internal approvals and (b) GCF has received a certificate or legal opinion from
the AE in form and substance satisfactory to the Fund confirming that all final internal
approvals by the AE have been obtained and that it has the authority and capacity to implement
the project.
83.
The proposed project will be implemented in Nepal, a country in which GCF is not
provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not
protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further
assessed. The Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities to the
na tional designated authority on 15 November 2019.
84.
The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have
both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or
investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant
privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are
made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and
expropriation in the country or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.
4. 7 List of proposed conditions (including legal)
85.

and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval,
confirming that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals needed by
it and has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed project;

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of
FP172
Proposal name: Mitigating GHG emission through modern, efficient and climate-
friendly clean cooking solutions (CCS)
Accredited entity: Alternative Energy Promotion Centre (AEPC)
Country/(ies): Nepal
Project/programme size: Small
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel
Scale: High
1 .1 Impact potential
1.
Nepal’s contribution to global historical and current greenhouse gas (GHG) emission is
very marginal. The country is however among the most vulnerable countries to climate change.
The Global Climate Risk Index 2020 ranked Nepal as the ninth most affected country to climate
change. Despite its marginal contribution to global GHG emissions, as reflected in its second
nationally determined contribution (NDC-2) Nepal is committed to acting on climate change in
line with the Paris Agreement. According to the country’s NDC-2 which was submitted to the
United Nation Framework Convention for Climate Change (UNFCCC) on 8 December 2020, the
Government of Nepal (GoN) believes that efforts to limit global average temperature rise to 1.5
°C would result in significantly lower risks for Nepal compared to an increase of 2 °C or more.
Nepal, therefore, calls on all Parties to increase their ambitions and move collectively onto
emission reduction pathways consistent with the ’1.5 °C warming limit in the Paris Agreement.
According to information in Nepal’s NDC-2, the country is also committed to delivering its own
contributions to global efforts to GHG emissions reduction in line with the international
principle of “common but differentiated responsibilities and respective capabilities”. The NDC-2
lists areas within Nepal’s economy where it intends to conditionally reduce its domestic GHG
emissions. The intention to reduce sectoral GHG emissions within the domestic activities in
Nepal conditional to having international support were spelled out in the NDC-2 and will cover
the following sectors: energy; agriculture, forestry, and land use (AFOLU). A significant
percentage of the GHG emission reductions that will be delivered by this intervention will come
from the energy sector and in particular: the increased greening of the power generation sector;
aggressive introduction of clean cooking solutions (CCS) in residential and commercial sectors;
an d introduction of e-transport.
2.
The proposed project submitted to GCF by the Alternative Energy Promotion Centre
(AEPC) and reviewed by the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) involves an
aggressive implementation of CCS in Nepal. The 2011 census in Nepal gave the following
statistics on cooking fuel use within households: 63 per cent use fuelwood; 10.38 per cent use
cow dung, while 21 per cent uses liquefied petroleum gas (LPG). Burning of solid fuels
(fuelwood and cow dung) to produce energy required for cooking in Nepal occurs in traditional
1
cookstoves (TCS). Burning of these traditional biomass fuels in TCS is not only very energy
inefficient, but also releases pa rticulates and noxious gases that are known to cause various
1
TCS have an efficiency of <10%. These stoves emits>18.3 g/MJ carbon monoxide and >1031 mg/MJ fine particulate
matter. Example: three-stone fire.

=== PAGE 133 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.04
Page 133
health problems in people (mostly women) who use these appliances to cook (mostly in indoor
areas). The combustion of that occurs with TCS also emits a massive amount of black carbon
and GHGs (such as carbon dioxide, methane). LPG, a fossil fuel is the other common cooking fuel
used in Nepal, and according to the information contained in the funding proposal reviewed by
the independent TAP, has been growing since the household capture of 21 per cent recorded in
the census of 2011. Accordingly, recent national household use of LPG for cooking in Nepal has
risen to about 40 per cent. The problem however is that not only does the combustion of LPG
2
emit GHG (CO ), but the fuel is also imported from India. According to statistics included in the
t
funding proposal, in the fiscal year 2019/2020, Nepal imported and consumed 449,063 M of
LPG. , Due to a widening trade deficit with India, Nepal has become very susceptible to supply
risk, with a very negative macroeconomic impact on the country’s economy. This presents a
double incentive to pursue a policy of phasing out LPG use in cooking to reduce GHG emission
footprint of the cooking sector as well as reducing or eliminating the negative impact on the
co untry’s economy.
3.
Nepal has a very low-carbon electricity grid, with more than 95 per cent of the power
generation connected to the grid consisting of low-carbon hydroelectric power plants. Of the
1,278 MW installed power capacity in 2020, slightly more than 1,250 MW (95.7 per cent) is
2
from hydroelectricity. Furthermore, Nepal has a significant potential for hydroelectricity,
estimated at around 83,000 MW. The claim that electricity supplied to the country’s grid is clean
2
and from a renewable energy source with no CO emissions was backed up by additional
information supplied to the independent TAP by the accredited entity (AE) in the course of this
rev iew. A summary of the information is presented as follows:
(a)
The hydroelectric capacity on the grid of Nepal is made of mostly of run-of- river (ROR)
and peaking run-of-river (PROR) capacities. ROR facilities do not have storage
capacities.
(b)
Clarifications and data presented to the independent TAP showed that apart from the
RORs and PRORs, other hydroelectric facilities connected to Nepal’s grid include the
Khulekhani–I and Khulekhani-II hydropower plants. Khulekhani–I is the only reservoir-
type hydro-electric power station in Nepal with installed capacity of 60 MW having two
units each with a capacity of 30 MW. Khulekhani-II hydropower station is a cascade of
Khulekhani-I with installed capacity of 32 MW.
(c)
PROR projects have on average, four to six hours daily peaking storage, while by design
ROR projects do not have any storage. Therefore, the absence of water storage of the
ROR coupled with the continuous flow in the ROR and the daily flushing in the PROR are
green by design.
(d)
An analysis of the power densities for the Khulekhani-I, Khulekhani-II as well as for the
PRORs (due to their peaking storage facility) showed that the power density for all these
2
hydroelectric plants was greater than 10 Watts/square meters (W/m ) According to the
guidance provided in the clean development mechanism large-scale consolidated
methodology on grid-connected electricity generation from renewable sources
(ACM0002), even the storage capacities, the project emission of these installed
hydroelectric generating facilities from water reservoir can be considered as zero.
(e)
These clarifications have been added to annex 22 of the funding proposal package to
provide clear evidence that emissions for Nepal’s grid electricity are zero.
4.
As already mentioned, the goal of AEPC for this submission is to introduce an aggressive
programme of CCS in Nepal to replace the status quo of cooking fuel end-use that includes,
traditional biomass (fuelwood and dung), and LPG. These will be replaced over a period of time
2
Department of Electricity Development, 2020. Operating p rojects available at < https://doed.gov.np/>

=== PAGE 134 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.04
Page 134
3
by CCS, which will include: tier 3+ improved cookstoves (ICS), biogas and electric cookstoves
(ECS). Electric cooking is included in the cohort of CCS in this programme based on the evidence
that electricity supply from the Nepalese grid is low carbon as described in paragraph 3 above.
4
In addition to this 80 per cent of Nepal’s population is connected to the national grid. In the
development of this proposed programme, AEPC took into consideration improvements made
relating to barriers that constrained the success of an earlier and similar programme. Prior to
the announcement of this present intervention, the GoN on its own, and in some cases in
collaboration with development partners, introduced CCS in the country. The Government and
external development partners (EDPs) invested around 4.5 million USD for CCS in the fiscal year
5
2018/2019. Predominantly, AEPC distributed mud ICS which had immediate positive impacts
bu t in the long run mud ICS are not durable and cannot provide a long-lasting impact.
5.
The reasons that have been attributed to the very low success rate of these earlier
interventions, having the same objectives as the present AEPC proposed project can be
su mmarized as follows:
(a)
The following barriers ensured that these programmes did not yield tangible results that
ar e expected when the status quo-ante cooking culture in the country is phased out:
(i)
Socioeconomic barriers to transition to CCS, which included consumer’s limited
awareness about the benefits of CCS, and subsidy policy on renewable energy
and reluctance to pay higher prices due to households’ low economic income;
(ii)
Technical barriers were also identified as reasons for the poor performance of
earlier CCS interventions in Nepal. The low level of technical knowledge and
unavailability of technicians are major constraints to the shift towards CCS. Very
few stove designs are available and consumers face a limited choice of models. In
addition, there is a dearth of domestic research and development that can
overcome the challenge of inefficient performance of cookstove models and
products for user requirements in Nepal. Since there are not many
manufacturers and vendors of tier 3+ ICS and ECS in Nepal, the cookers had to
be imported from India and China. In addition to the low domestic capacity to
manufacture CCS stoves in-country, there is also evidence of limited technical
workforce availability in many areas of Nepal, coupled with little involvement of
women;
(iii)
Thirdly, financial barriers also constrained the performance of earlier
interventions. Most of the TCS users are among the poorest households, who
lack the needed capital to switch to CCS technology and they usually do not have
access to finance. Although a few cooperatives provide loans for CCS at
community level, the interest rate is usually too high for the poorest households;
(iv)
Weak institutional infrastructure is another barrier that has been identified as a
key reason for the failure of earlier CCS interventions to achieve set goals. The
new Constitution of Nepal gave the mandate for the development of renewable
energy to the local governments. However, their institutional set-up is weak as
regards the necessary technologies. They also lack: technical and financial
capacities; awareness on the impact of clean energy; dedicated human
resources; and e xpertise and experience required for planning (energy plans),
3
Tier 3+ ICS has an efficiency of ≥30 per cent. These stoves emit ≤7.2 g/MJ carbon monoxide and ≤218 mg/MJ fine
particulate matter.
4
Ministry of Finance, 2020. Economic Survey 2076/2077. Available at
<https://mof.gov.np/uploads/document/file/Economic_Survey_2076-77.pdf>
5
AEPC, 2018. Budget of FY 2075-76.https://www.aepc.gov.np/uploads/docs/2018-07-
09Budget%20of%20FY %202075-76.pdf ; AEPC, 2019. Progress at Glance: A year in Review FY 2075/76 (2018/19).
https://www.aepc.gov.np/uploads /docs/progress-at-glan ce-a-year-in-review-fy-207576-201819-1572879503.pdf

=== PAGE 135 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.04
Page 135
procuring (demand aggregation and bulk tendering approach), implementing
and monitoring renewable energy technologies projects and programmes;
(v)
Although e-stoves were not included in the earlier CCS interventions, the
barriers that are likely to limit the success of e-stove adoption in the country
were also discussed in the funding proposal. Key barriers that are expected to
hinder the utilization of e-stoves in the present intervention are expected to
include: poor quality of electricity supply in general in many parts of Nepal and
voltage fluctuations in many parts of the Nepalese grid. In addition, although the
national average electricity access is about 80 per cent, there are still areas
where grid access is still low and this will inevitably limit achievement of the
objectives for e-stoves. It is also important to ensure that end users can afford
the prices of e-stoves;
(vi)
Another set of barriers that did not allow earlier interventions to deliver
tangible results was the bureaucratic approach used to roll out the CCS. The
approach had the following essential elements: setting up an annual target for
technology deployment; fixing a certain amount of subsidy for each technology
type for households and institutions in groups of districts; and shortlisting of
companies (who will supply the CCS equipment) based on a set of qualification
criteria to implement those targets. These companies are referred to as pre-
qualified companies (PQs): they collect demand from households and
institutions and submit installation completion forms to the project manager
(PM); the PM, who is a GoN agent, then carries out sample-based monitoring to
release the final installment of the subsidy amount to PQs. This approach was
found to be bureaucratic and lengthy, and since the price of the technology, and
hence the subsidy, was pre-determined by the PM, there was no incentive for the
PQs to reduce the cost of technology or propose more innovative and better
technology;
(b)
Actions and strategies that aim to address the identified barriers to achieving a
successful CCS project have been incorporated into the currently proposed intervention
an d can be summarized as follows:
(i)
To address the bureaucratic process that did not facilitate earlier attempts at
implementing a CCS process in Nepal, the following intervention structure has
be en built into the proposed project:
(1)
A more cost-effective process that involves bulk tendering via reverse
auctioning, which is expected to de-risk the investment by introducing
output-based financing;
(2)
Adopting an implementation process that has a built-in quality assurance
procedure with adequate after-sales service. This would include
elements of funding to strengthen existing test centres and expand
services at the regional and local levels; and
(3)
Inclusion of local governments in the process to play crucial roles in
improving access to CCS through the development of their capacities;
(ii)
Project design and implementation planning and procedures for the proposed
CCS project included the following elements to address the socioeconomic
ba rriers already discussed:
(1)
Funding of project elements that have been designed to bring about
behavioural changes among users. For example, such elements involve
programmes to raise the awareness of household users regarding the

=== PAGE 136 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.04
Page 136
cost and health benefits of ICS through campaigns and promotional
activities, stressing gender parity and empowerment of women;
(2)
Implementation of the project activities in 1 million households in the
Terai Region of Nepal during a 5-year period with a strong focus on: pre-
and post-purchase behaviour and adequate enforcement of
implementation rules that will enable the households to switch to the
proposed technologies on their own accord given the behavioural
changes coupled with intervention on value chain through awareness
and promotional campaigns, promotion of emerging CCS technologies,
training and support with enhanced repair and maintenance services
and technology availability;
(iii)
Actions built into the implementation of this new CCS project are incorporated

(1)

(2)
Strengthening of existing test centres to establish the quality of all the

(3)
Addressing of gender issues on technological aspects through focused
development of women’s involvement in relevant components of the
value chain;
(4)
Promotion of electric cooking in grid connected households through
incentives, which will include the introduction of cost-effective tariff
structure for household consumption;
(5)
Ensuring that all households selected to participate in the programme

(6)
Careful and unambiguous selection of the beneficiaries who will include
selected clusters in rural settings and selected clusters in urban and peri-
urban areas;
(7)
AEPC will provide Nepal Electricity Authority (NEA) with beneficiary
mapping so that the electricity utility can upgrade capacitors and
conductors in overloaded areas. In terms of the medium- and long-term
measures, AEPC will work with NEA on increasing distribution
transformer size and improving distribution and transmission lines to
control voltage fluctuations in the project areas;
(8)
Through the proposed project, the government will play a major role in
setting up reliable infrastructure which will assure the wider usage of
electric cooking among households;
(9)
Electric cookstoves with strong reliability have been proposed in the CCS
project in order to diminish the usage of LPG in the country;
(iv)
Actions built into the implementation of the new CCS project to overcome the
financial barriers that prevented earlier interventions from being effective
inc lude:
(1)
Bulk tendering via reverse auctioning that should reduce the price of CCS
through competition;

=== PAGE 137 ===

Page 137
(2)
The concessional GCF debt and the grants, coupled with counterpart
funding in the form of equity from the GoN and funds from other
development partners would help to mitigate the financial barriers;
(3)
The procurement will take the approach of bulk tendering via reverse
auctioning (selection based on least cost) for cost effectiveness and
output-based payment to de-risk the investment;
(v)
Some of the actions included in implementation of the programme to overcome

wi ll include:
(1)
Empowering of LGs through their engagement in several aspects of the

implementation phase; and
(2)
The proposed project includes elements that will build the capacity of
LGs and provincial governments (PGs) to prioritize CCS promotion in
their periodic and annual planning, formulating appropriate policies and
mechanisms.
6.
It is expected that the inclusion of these various barrier mitigation elements, derived
from previous lessons learned, will facilitate the success of the intervention now submitted to
th e GCF Secretariat for Board approval.
7.
The project will be implemented in the Terai Region of Nepal.Teraiis bordere. d by the
Indo-Gangetic plain in the south and the Churia physiographic region in the north. As in other
parts of Nepal, penetration of clean cooking solution technologies is very low in Terai According
6
to the 2011 census, In Terai 56 per cent of households used fuelwood, 22 per cent cow dung
and 15 per cent used LPG. Recent statistics indicate that fuel-wood consumption in Terai has
remained constant, while LPG consumption is increasing, with about 40 per cent of households
using it. It is stated in the funding proposal that access to grid electricity in Nepal is about 80 per
cent of the population and that the Terai region has a relatively higher access rate. However, use
of electricity for cooking has a low rate of adoption, as merely 0.08 per cent of the target
7
po pulation relies on it.

11.
According to the AE as presented in the funding proposal, inclusion of the local
government (LG) tier in this project is an innovative inclusion that has not been tried in similar
initiatives in the past. Mainstreaming and capacitating local governments in the renewable
energy sector is one of the three innovative concepts the AEPC has built into the project to
increase the use of clean cooking solutions in Nepal. This will ensure grassroots inclusion in the
programme. Strong participation by LGs in the project is shown through the provision of grant
funding for the activities of Component 1. The LGs will contribute 14.37 per cent (USD
7,065,442) of the total project budget as co-financing. The LG contribution to the budget of
Component 1 is 18 per cent, while GCF grant funding for this component constitutes 33 per cent
and co-financing from AEPC is 49 per cent. It has been clearly stated in the funding proposal
that the LG contribution is not in-kind but actual payment that will be guided by a memorandum
of understanding between the LGs and AEPC that will stipulate the disbursement structure.
Furthermore, the LG co-financing contribution will .be paid out directly by LGs to private
tec hnology providers when the installation targets in the respective LGs are verified.

1. 2 Paradigm shift potential
15.
The paradigm shift potential of this CCS project in Nepal is discussed considering the
extent to which the project can catalyse impacts beyond the proposed intervention. Previous
clean cooking solutions in Nepal were not successful as already discussed and to avoid the same
errors and barriers certain changes must be made in the process. Potential paradigm shifts are
either built into the implementation process or will be delivered by the project; these are
d 1. i 2 s .1 c . u ssePda braeldoiwgm- shift in the procurement process
16.
As stated earlier, inherent barriers have prevented the achievement of expected results
in earlier projects of this nature. One of these was the lengthy and inefficient process for
procurement of clean cookstoves, with bureaucracy hindering cost-effectiveness. In the project
proposed for GCF funding for, the concept of bulk tendering via reverse auctioning, along with
an output-based financing model was introduced and is a new procurement process in Nepal.
The aggregation of the demand for CCS that will be deployed will grow the concept of bulk
tendering, while the reverse auctioning method will significantly reduce the cost of CCS
technologies compared to the status quo prior to tendering. Moreover, the output-based
financing modality will incentivize the vendors, who are private sector entities, by lowering
their risk. This process will also engender multiple check and balance mechanisms to ensure
quality assurance and de-risk project investment. The success of this proposed procurement
approach should incentivize a change in the business-as-usual public procurement process in
N 1.2 e . p 2. a l inP goetneenrtaial. l for scaling up
17.
The use of biomass (fuelwood and dung) and LPG as cooking fuels is a source of GHG
emissions as well as particulates and noxious gases. GHG gases are the focus of climate change
mitigation while noxious gases and particulates are well known to negatively impact the health
of the users of the TCSs especially in an indoor environment where cooking is mostly done.
Women are clearly more severely impacted as the principle users of TCS. Recognition of these
problems has catalyzed various CCS interventions by both the GoN and development partners.
However, most of the efforts made to date cannot be scaled up. The proposed CCS project was
designed with built-in lessons from these previous efforts and new strategies will mitigate the
known barriers. Robust results will support replication of this project in other parts of Terai,
an d other regions currently not involved in the intervention.
18.
The second nationally determined contribution (NDC) of Nepal indicated a national goal
for CCS for by 2030. Currently, electric cooking accounts for only 5 per cent and the second NDC
aims to ensure that 25 per cent of households use electric stoves as their primary mode of
cooking by 2030. The project design has a good potential to contribute significantly to the
achievement of this target through a strengthened supply chain. Promotion of the use of e-
cookstoves, tier 3+ ICS and biogas stoves should lead to their proliferation across the country
wi thout external interventions even after the project period.
19.
The adopted procurement process is expected to deliver more cost-effective CCS in
Nepal. The impact of this will be felt not only in the affordability of the cooking technologies
adopted but also in the quality of these solutions. The involvement of LGs, the administrative
l
1
e
.2
v
.3
e
.
l cloIsnensto tvoa tthioen p opulation should also offer potential for the scaling up the project.

20.
Innovation is partially delivered through changes to the status quo that result in value
creation, reforms and renovation of failing systems, and ensuring sustainability of the process.
Many of the implementation components of the CCS currently proposed are innovative and will
deliver a paradigm shift in the plan of the GoN to expand the use of CCSs in the country.
Compared to the business-as-usual scenario, the procurement plan is an example of one such
innovation and it is foreseen that it will deliver a better result compared to the bureaucratic and
inefficient system which discouraged the involvement of the private sector. Strengthening of the
q

21.
Potential for knowledge-sharing and learning is well built into the project
implementation. Empirical knowledge will be generated through project monitoring of the bulk
tendering, reverse auctioning and output-based financing. Component 2 of the implementation
also covers development of a management information system, which will serve as a national
monitoring system with real-time data collection from LGs. This will offer good potential for
knowledge-sharing and provide information that will serve as a learning platform during
im plementation of the CCS intervention and replication in other regions of Nepal.
22.
Various activities included in Component 3 of the implementation scheme will provide
platforms for knowledge-sharing and learning. These will include: national stakeholders’
consultation workshops; annual national-level reviews and consultations; capacity-building
activities; and various training and skill development activities. All these will ensure that the
po tential for knowledge-sharing and learning of the proposal is robust.
23.
The proposed project will deliver comprehensive and innovative attributes that the
independent TAP believes will contribute strongly to a high paradigm shift potential. It will
deliver an enabling environment that will make the tangible outcomes of the intervention
sustainable, while also enhancing replicability of the project concept. The knowledge-sharing
platform, which is a sub-component of the project delivery, will enhance robust knowledge-
sharing and learning attributes that are essential for the scalability and replication of the
project. In the light of these observations, the independent TAP concludes that the paradigm
shift potential is high.
Scale: High

(a)

(d)

fuels that will be displaced by the CCS technologies of this intervention. LPG is imported
from India and China. The project is expected to reduce about 30,000 tons of LPG
imported from India and China, representing a foreign currency saving of about USD

11.4.4.1. NNeeeedds so fo tfh teh ceo urnetcriyp ient
30.
Nepal is highly vulnerable to climate change. The country is ranked as the ninth most
affected by climate change, as per the Global Climate Risk Index 2020. Although it contributes
very marginally to global GHG emissions, the country’s climate policies and strategies are allied
with the global agreement to take more aggressive steps towards reducing the negative impacts
of climate change. Nepal is therefore committed to taking active actions to mitigate climate
change impacts both through its own efforts and conditional on availability of international
efforts. Nepal, therefore, calls on all Parties to increase their ambitions and move collectively
onto emission reduction pathways consistent with the 1.5 °C warming limit in the Paris
Agreement. Therefore, the proposed CCS intervention is fulfilling a need of the country for
climate action, which will deliver tangible GHG emissions reduction with grant funding from
GC F and from the GoN.
31.
At baseline, access to clean energy cooking solutions is a massive problem for Nepal. A
large percentage of households in Nepal depend on traditional emissive energy sources, such as
firewood, animal waste and crop residues for cooking pu
1
r
1
poses. Currently, about 73.5 pe r cent
of Nepalese households depend on firewood for cooking using very inefficient traditional
12
cookstoves (TCS). A common fuel used in TCS is animal waste in the form of dung cake. Dung
cake is the most polluting fuel for cooking and is therefore at the bottom of the energy ladder.
Dung cakes are produced at the household level and burnt in traditional open fires or mud
stoves, resulting in the emission of a dangerous cocktail of hundreds of pollutants to which
women and young children are exposed daily. Given that the CCS proposal aims to replace the
TCS with efficient and less emitting tier 3+ ICS then the proposed project will meet the need to
phase out TCS as described in many of the Government’s planning documents (e.g. NDCs). The
project will therefore contribute to the Government’s strategy to reduce GHG emissions as well
as improving indoor air quality. The World Health Organization estimates that there are 8,700
deaths a year from indoor air pollution in Nepal. The project will also meet the need of Nepal to
ph ase out the use of dung as a cooking fuel in TCS.
32.
Another fuel used in household cooking at baseline is LPG. While fuelwood is the most
common household cooking fuel in the rural areas of Nepal, LPG is the most used cooking fuel in
the urban areas. LPG is smoke free and does not emit the cocktail of indoor pollution produced
when biomass fuel is burnt. It is however a fossil fuel, which when combusted emits GHGs.
Given the global plan to appreciably limit GHG emissions from fossil fuels, it is the intention of
the GoN to reduce the use of LPG in household cooking. In addition LPG is a costly import for
t
Nepal from China and India. In 2018–19, Nepal imported 429,609 M of LPG, which is a 270 per
13
cent increase since 2008/09. It is the intention of the Government to curb this increasing
dependence on LPG to reduce the country’s supply risk (as witnessed during the 2015 fuel
crisis). Therefore, the inclusion of LPG substitution in the proposed project meets a highly
im portant need for Nepal.
33.
A key alternative in the proposed project is the displacement of LPG as a cooking fuel in
Nepal by electric cooking. This will be based on the grid electricity supply which is low carbon.
A sizeable percentage of grid-connected electricity generation plants in Nepal are hydroelectric
11
World Bank, 2019. NEPAL | Beyond Connections: Energy Access Diagnostic Report Based on the Multi-Tier
Framework
12
ibid
13
See Page 11 of the FP under Section B

=== PAGE 146 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.04
Page 146
power plants, produced mainly with run of river (RoR) systems as well as some peaking run of
river (PRoR) capacities connected to the grid. PROR projects have an average of four to six
hours daily peaking storage, while ROR by design do not have any storage. Both systems are
green in that they do not emit GHG after they have been commissioned. The other hydroelectric
facilities connected to the grid of Nepal include the Khulekhani–I and Khulekhani-II hydropower
plants. Khulekhani–I with a total installed capacity of 60 MW has storage, while Khulekhani–II is
a cascade of Khulekhani–I. The AE provided quantitative information on the power density of
Khulekhani–I and the PRoR during submission of the independent TAP evaluation. The
information on the power density of Khulekhani–I showed that it does not emit GHGs from its
water storage dams. Thus there is valid evidence that electricity used for the e-cookstoves will
not lead to GHG emissions. Thus, the inclusion of electric cookstoves in household CCS meets the
n 1. e 4. e 2 d . s ofE Ncoenpoalm aisc n aontedd f iinn asnocmiael o nf etehde sn oatfi tohnea lc poluannntriny g documents.
34.
It can be concluded that the financial structuring of the project has been designed in a
way that meets the country’s economic and financial needs. The economy of Nepal in recent
years has been suffering from fiscal weakness due to fiscal deficit from imports of LPG from
India and China. Nepal being a least developed country and one of the most vulnerable countries
to climate change, the project aims to address the vulnerability and exposure to climate risk and
considers the economic and social development level of the beneficiaries. Thus, the
procurement policy for the CCS will ensure the cost-effectiveness of the process, which will
benefit the private sector entities involved in the project. The low-income users who will pay for
the project equipment will also benefit; a survey of the Nepalese people carried out in 2018
shows that in the Terai region, the average monthly income of households can be as low as USD
250 per annum. The proposed project design will help to achieve the GoN aim to provide CCS
e 1. q 4 u .3 i . pmeAnbts teon lcoew o-ifn aclotmeren haotiuvsee shoouldrsc.e s of financing
35.
It can be argued that since previous efforts made to introduce CCS to Nepal attracted
funding from both the GoN and international development agencies present in the country, the
failure of these programmes was due to inadequate funding. These previous interventions were
mostly funded by the GoN and inclusion of additional grant funding from GCF will help to
a 1. c 4 h .4 i . e ve Ntheee dovs efroarl ls gtroeanlsg othf tehnei nCgC Sin psrtoitjeuctti.o ns and implementation capacity
36.
The need to strengthen institutions in Nepal to ensure the success of the proposed CCS
cannot be overemphasized. The intervention aims to include LGs, giving them a strong role in
the project, thus extending institutional strengthening.
37.
Given the analysis of the project information made available for the review of this
project submission, and the discussion of the issues presented in this section, the independent
TAP concludes that Nepal’s CCS project will deliver the estimated decarbonization of the
household cooking sector, while incentivizing the shift to CCS, including using low-carbon grid
electricity for cooking. This is expected to deliver a robust economic and financial scenario that
will make the project concept successful. Therefore, the independent TAP has scored the needs
of the recipient as high.
Scale: High
11.5.5.1. CEvoiudenntcrey o of wconuenrtrsyh oipw nersh ip thro ugh par ticipat ion of r elevant stakeholder s

=== PAGE 147 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.04
Page 147
38.
Country ownership is strongly related to national stakeholder engagement with the
goals and objectives of the proposed project. The funding proposal provides the following
information on the level of stakeholder engagement adopted by the accredited entity (AE) and
the Government of Nepal (GoN)in the proposed CCS project. These key issues that we believe
pr ovide a strong basis for country ownership are summarized as follows:
(a)
The CCS project has been planned within the context of the GoN’s historical commitment
to international climate change agreements. The country has a long tradition of being at
the forefront of combating climate change. The country’s institutions have public policy
institutional arrangements, which are delivering various strategies that may be
co nsidered precursors of the proposed CCS project. The relevant strategies include:
(i)
An Investment Prospectus and Biomass Energy Strategy was endorsed by the
GoN in 2017. This prospectus and strategy aims at attaining clean cooking
solutions for all in two stages, namely. by improved solutions for cooking by
2022 (no traditional cooking using open fire burning), and provision of modern
cooking solutions with at least tier 3+ level by 2030;
14
(ii)
Nepal’s NDC, which has as one of its climate change strategies the reduction of
by 50 per cent of fossil fuel dependency by 2050 and equipping households in
rural areas with smokeless kitchens;
(iii)
The Fifteenth Plan (2019/20 to 2023/24) that gives high priority to household
biogas, ICS, and production of bio-briquettes and pellets; and
(iv)
The climate change policy of 2019, which aims to contribute to the
socioeconomic prosperity of the nation by building a climate resilient society.
The policy has been introduced with the objective of providing policy guidance
to various levels and thematic areas to develop a resilient society by reducing
the risk of climate change impacts. A relevant key strategy of this policy is the
requirement that low emission technologies and adaptation programmes will be
incorporated in settlement development plans, which would mean CCS
technology would be a key priority.
(b)
As stated in the funding proposal and its annexes, the proposed CCS project was
developed with the full consultation of all relevant stakeholders, including
representatives of indigenous peoples.
(c)
The high degree of participation by the LGs as a project stakeholder with funding
contribution responsibilities is an indication of strong grassroot stakeholders’

Other key performance indices of the financing scheme can be summarized as follows:

as the NPV is negative (USD 38.6 million).

1 Introduction
1.1 Gender Background
The International Women's Year (1975) and the United Nations Decade for Women (1976-85)
created a wave of activity for bringing women in the development mainstream worldwide. Post this,
a series of the status of women in Nepal studies were carried out throughout 1779-81. The policy to
promote women's participation in development appeared for the first time in the Sixth National Plan
(1980-85) of the country.1 Nepal has accessed Convention on Political Rights for women on April 26,
1966, followed by ratification of Convention on the Elimination of All forms of Discrimination against
Women (CEDAW) on April 22, 1991. Nepal has also ratified Optional Protocol to the Convention on
the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women on June 15, 2007, signed the Beijing
Platform for Action, and the International Conference on Population and Development +10. The
treaties established a way forward to mainstream Gender Issues in state laws and policies.
The Constitution of Nepal promulgated in 2015, with the commitment to end gender discrimination,
has guaranteed the equal right to property, right to safe motherhood and reproductive health rights,
right to ensure proportional representation at all concerned levels as well as the right to enjoy
fundamental human rights for all women. A separate code of conduct for the prevention of violence
against women and girls has been introduced as the Sexual Harassment at Workplace Prevention Act
2015 Also, the Criminal (Penal Code) 2017 protects women and children, particularly minorities.
Women Development Programme (WDP) created in 1981 as a women development office with a
vision for an equitable and prosperous society and prime strategies on mainstreaming,
empowerment, and inclusion in the domain of Women’s development. Since 2000, WDP has been
operating with nationwide coverage. For gender issues and women empowerment, the functional
ministry is the Ministry of Women, Children and Senior Citizen (MoWCSC). The Department of
Women and Children under MoWCSC was created in 2000 and later reformed in 2010 with a
mandate to do functional jobs in Women’s development. National Women Commission was founded
in 2001 as a constitutional body to protect women’s rights, women’s welfare, and incorporate
women in developmental mainstreams and establish women’s rights and gender equity.
Institutional Mechanisms for GESI Policy in Ministries, National Commissions for Women, Dalits,
Indigenous Nationalities, Madhesis, Muslims, Tharus and a National Inclusion Commission that is
mandated to protect the rights of Khas Aryas, Pichardiaka (“backward”) class, persons with
disabilities, senior citizens, labourers, peasants, minority and marginalized communities, people of
the Karnali, and the indigent class, National Planning Commission, MoWCSC, and the Department of
Women and Children in Central Level, with similar units in Provincial Level. GESI Mainstreaming
Facilitation in Local Level is in process.
Nepal Demographic and Health Survey (NDHS) had conducted a survey in 2016 which provides
reliable estimates at the national level, for urban and rural areas, 3 ecological zones, 5 development
regions, and 7 provinces. It has summarised the status of women empowerment in three
categories.2.
a. Employment: 68% of married women were employed in past 12 months compared to 97% of
married women
b. Ownership of assets: 8% own a home, and 11% own land alone or jointly; among men, 19%
own a home and 21% own land alone or jointly
1 Department of Women and Children, Nepal https://www.dwc.gov.np/pages/history
2 https://kathmandupost.com/national/2017/04/24/presidents-women-empowerment-programme-
starts-in-26-districts
4

c. Participation in household decisions: 53% to make decisions about major household
purchases
Separate governmental programmes such as ‘The President's Women empowerment programme’
started on March 07, 2017 to establish a gender-responsive governance system and a just society by
developing and promoting women’s socio-economic empowerment and entrepreneurship.3 There
are many parallel women empowerment programmes conducted at national and local levels by
several NGOs.
The ministry of Federal affairs and Local development had introduced a Gender Equality and Social
Inclusion Policy in 2009 as an uptake in women empowerment in social inclusivity in Local
Development entities. The current 15th national plan has allocated the budget of NRs. 14.98 Billion
to mainstream Gender equality and inclusion (GESI) principles into the governance system of
provincial and local levels as an expected outcome of Provincial and Local government support
programmes from 2019/2020 to 2023/24.
The current 15th national plan has the gender equality and women empowerment vision to “Build
Nepal as a gender-equal nation.” It has identified the target indicators to fulfill in the year 2023/24
with reference to baseline data from 2018/19 tabulated below. 4

Human Development Report 20196 shows that the Gender Inequality Index (GII) value of Nepal
is 0.476, ranking it 115 out of 162 countries in the 2018 index. Whereas, 2018 female Human
Development Index (HDI) value for Nepal is 0.549 in contrast with 0.612 for males, resulting in a
